People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Yeah… um, I’m here because I scared myself, honestly. I’ve been having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Not even saying it out loud feels right, but that’s what’s been going through my head.

I tried to keep it to myself at first, because I don’t like making a big thing out of things, and I’m not real used to talking about this kind of stuff. But it kept sitting there in my mind, and it got to where I told my supervisor, because I guess I knew I shouldn’t just keep pretending it was nothing. That’s what brought me in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I usually have little things I fool with, you know? I’ve got tomato plants I’ll mess around with, check on them, pull weeds, see how they’re coming along. I’ve got a bench where I’ll sand wood, just kind of work with my hands, pass the time. I’ll sit and watch the Falcons even when they’re making me mad, and after church I normally stay around and talk to folks.

But lately… I don’t know. I look at all that stuff and it’s like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I usually care about, but I can’t seem to get myself into them. I’ll stand there looking at the plants or the wood on the bench and just feel nothing much about it. Even football, even talking after church, I just don’t have that pull toward it like I normally do.

Yeah… I think so, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything like that. I just notice it more with eating. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.

Even food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. So I wouldn’t know exactly how many pounds or anything, but my eating’s definitely been off.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.

At work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.

And when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.

Getting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.

That’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now, I’d say. It kind of started after my daughter left Georgia for the military. That was when I noticed this whole change in me, like something shifted, and those thoughts started coming in after that. It hasn’t been years or anything like that, but these last couple weeks have felt pretty heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you normally like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I usually have little things I fool with, you know? I’ve got tomato plants I’ll mess around with, check on them, pull weeds, see how they’re coming along. I’ve got a bench where I’ll sand wood, just kind of work with my hands, pass the time. I’ll sit and watch the Falcons even when they’re making me mad, and after church I normally stay around and talk to folks.\n\nBut lately… I don’t know. I look at all that stuff and it’s like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I usually care about, but I can’t seem to get myself into them. I’ll stand there looking at the plants or the wood on the bench and just feel nothing much about it. Even football, even talking after church, I just don’t have that pull toward it like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I mean, I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that, if that makes sense. Some nights I lay there and it feels like I’m just kind of watching the dark, but I couldn’t tell you exactly how many hours I’m getting or whether it’s every single night.\n\nI know I haven’t felt right lately, but with sleep specifically, I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer. My nights have felt off, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it a real sleep problem or just my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing things over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I’ve got things I normally enjoy — little things, but they’re mine, you know? I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, see if they needed water, pull a few weeds. I’d sit at my bench and sand wood, just work with my hands for a while. I’d watch the Falcons, even if I was fussing at the TV half the game. And after church, I’d usually hang around and talk to folks instead of rushing off.\n\nBut lately it’s like… I look at all that and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I’m supposed to care about, because I always have, but I don’t really get anything from them right now. I’ll stand there by the plants or look at the wood sitting there, and there’s just no pull to it. No pleasure in it like there used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything like that. I just notice it more with eating. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.\n\nEven food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. So I wouldn’t know exactly how many pounds or anything, but my eating’s definitely been off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done than usual over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAt work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.\n\nAnd when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.\n\nGetting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I have. I don’t know if guilty is exactly the word, but maybe it is. I keep going back over my daughter leaving, over and over, like I’m trying to find the one place where I should’ve done something different.

I’ll be sitting there and my mind just starts in — like, maybe I didn’t listen enough, or maybe I was too hard on her, or maybe I missed something plain as day that a better father would’ve seen. It’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. I don’t even know what I think I could fix now, but I keep blaming myself anyway.

But it kept sitting there in my mind, and it got to where I told my supervisor, because I guess I knew I shouldn’t just keep pretending it was nothing. It hasn’t been years or anything like that, but these last couple weeks have felt pretty heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you normally like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I usually have little things I fool with, you know? I’ve got tomato plants I’ll mess around with, check on them, pull weeds, see how they’re coming along. I’ve got a bench where I’ll sand wood, just kind of work with my hands, pass the time. I’ll sit and watch the Falcons even when they’re making me mad, and after church I normally stay around and talk to folks.\n\nBut lately… I don’t know. I look at all that stuff and it’s like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I usually care about, but I can’t seem to get myself into them. I’ll stand there looking at the plants or the wood on the bench and just feel nothing much about it. I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that, if that makes sense. My nights have felt off, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it a real sleep problem or just my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing things over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I’ve got things I normally enjoy — little things, but they’re mine, you know? I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, see if they needed water, pull a few weeds. I’d sit at my bench and sand wood, just work with my hands for a while. I’d watch the Falcons, even if I was fussing at the TV half the game. And after church, I’d usually hang around and talk to folks instead of rushing off.\n\nBut lately it’s like… I look at all that and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. I’ll stand there by the plants or look at the wood sitting there, and there’s just no pull to it. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.\n\nEven food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. So I wouldn’t know exactly how many pounds or anything, but my eating’s definitely been off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done than usual over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAt work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.\n\nAnd when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.\n\nGetting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t know if guilty is exactly the word, but maybe it is. I keep going back over my daughter leaving, over and over, like I’m trying to find the one place where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI’ll be sitting there and my mind just starts in — like, maybe I didn’t listen enough, or maybe I was too hard on her, or maybe I missed something plain as day that a better father would’ve seen. And I know people can say grown children make their own choices and all that, and I understand that in my head, but it doesn’t stop me from feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nIt’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. Yeah… that’s actually part of why I’m here.

I don’t know that I’d say I’ve been, like, making a plan or anything like that… but I keep having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it. And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.

At first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. It scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor, because I didn’t really trust myself to just keep pretending it was nothing.

And I know people can say grown children make their own choices and all that, and I understand that in my head, but it doesn’t stop me from feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nIt’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. Like if I went to sleep and that was it. And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.\n\nAt first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, no, not really. That’s been one of the stranger parts, because these are things I’ve always had, you know? Little things, but they were mine.

I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, tie them up right, see if any were coming in. I could spend a good while out there and not even think about it. Lately I’ll look out there and see they need attention, and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I ought to care, but I just… don’t feel much of anything.

Same with my workbench. I used to like sanding wood, getting a piece smooth, just standing there with the dust and the smell of it. That used to settle me some. Now I’ll go out there, pick something up, and just put it back down. It feels like too much, or like there’s no point to it.

Even watching the Falcons — I used to make time for that. I’d holler at the TV like anybody else, get into it. Now it’ll be on and I’m barely watching. I don’t get worked up, don’t really enjoy it.

And after church, I used to stay around talking with people, catching up, shaking hands, asking how folks were doing. Lately I just want to leave. Or I stand there and feel like I’m not really part of it. All of that stuff just feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.

I’ll sit and watch the Falcons even when they’re making me mad, and after church I normally stay around and talk to folks.\n\nBut lately… I don’t know. I look at all that stuff and it’s like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I usually care about, but I can’t seem to get myself into them. I’ll stand there looking at the plants or the wood on the bench and just feel nothing much about it. Even football, even talking after church, I just don’t have that pull toward it like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I mean, I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that, if that makes sense. Some nights I lay there and it feels like I’m just kind of watching the dark, but I couldn’t tell you exactly how many hours I’m getting or whether it’s every single night.\n\nI know I haven’t felt right lately, but with sleep specifically, I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer. My nights have felt off, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it a real sleep problem or just my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing things over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I’ve got things I normally enjoy — little things, but they’re mine, you know? I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, see if they needed water, pull a few weeds. I’d sit at my bench and sand wood, just work with my hands for a while. I’d watch the Falcons, even if I was fussing at the TV half the game. And after church, I’d usually hang around and talk to folks instead of rushing off.\n\nBut lately it’s like… I look at all that and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I’m supposed to care about, because I always have, but I don’t really get anything from them right now. I’ll stand there by the plants or look at the wood sitting there, and there’s just no pull to it. No pleasure in it like there used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything like that. I just notice it more with eating. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.\n\nEven food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. So I wouldn’t know exactly how many pounds or anything, but my eating’s definitely been off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done than usual over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAt work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.\n\nAnd when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.\n\nGetting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t know if guilty is exactly the word, but maybe it is. I keep going back over my daughter leaving, over and over, like I’m trying to find the one place where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI’ll be sitting there and my mind just starts in — like, maybe I didn’t listen enough, or maybe I was too hard on her, or maybe I missed something plain as day that a better father would’ve seen. And I know people can say grown children make their own choices and all that, and I understand that in my head, but it doesn’t stop me from feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nIt’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. I don’t even know what I think I could fix now, but I keep blaming myself anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about actually harming yourself over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually part of why I’m here.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I’ve been, like, making a plan or anything like that… but I keep having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it. And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.\n\nAt first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. It scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor, because I didn’t really trust myself to just keep pretending it was nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest in activities that you normally find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, no, not really. That’s been one of the stranger parts, because these are things I’ve always had, you know? Little things, but they were mine.\n\nI used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, tie them up right, see if any were coming in. I could spend a good while out there and not even think about it. Lately I’ll look out there and see they need attention, and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I ought to care, but I just… don’t feel much of anything.\n\nSame with my workbench. I used to like sanding wood, getting a piece smooth, just standing there with the dust and the smell of it. That used to settle me some. Now I’ll go out there, pick something up, and just put it back down. It feels like too much, or like there’s no point to it.\n\nEven watching the Falcons — I used to make time for that. I’d holler at the TV like anybody else, get into it. Now it’ll be on and I’m barely watching. I don’t get worked up, don’t really enjoy it.\n\nAnd after church, I used to stay around talking with people, catching up, shaking hands, asking how folks were doing. Lately I just want to leave. Or I stand there and feel like I’m not really part of it. All of that stuff just feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F32.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.\n\nAt first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. It scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor, because I didn’t really trust myself to just keep pretending it was nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest in activities that you normally find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, no, not really. That’s been one of the stranger parts, because these are things I’ve always had, you know? Little things, but they were mine.\n\nI used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, tie them up right, see if any were coming in. I could spend a good while out there and not even think about it. Lately I’ll look out there and see they need attention, and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I ought to care, but I just… don’t feel much of anything.\n\nSame with my workbench. I used to like sanding wood, getting a piece smooth, just standing there with the dust and the smell of it. That used to settle me some. Now I’ll go out there, pick something up, and just put it back down. It feels like too much, or like there’s no point to it.\n\nEven watching the Falcons — I used to make time for that. I’d holler at the TV like anybody else, get into it. Now it’ll be on and I’m barely watching. I don’t get worked up, don’t really enjoy it.\n\nAnd after church, I used to stay around talking with people, catching up, shaking hands, asking how folks were doing. Lately I just want to leave. Or I stand there and feel like I’m not really part of it. All of that stuff just feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been experiencing these thoughts of your family being better off without you nearly every day over the past two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I didn't wake up, and that scared me enough to tell my supervisor.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… pretty close to that. I don’t know if I can say I’ve counted every single day, like marked it on a calendar, but it’s been most days, enough that it feels like it’s always sitting right there.

That thought about them maybe being better off if I didn’t wake up… it keeps coming back. Sometimes it’s first thing in the morning, sometimes at night when everything’s quiet, and sometimes it just hits me in the middle of doing nothing much. And I hate even saying that, because I don’t want to put that on my family, but that’s the thought I’ve been having.

That’s really why I came in. It scared me. I got to where I didn’t want to just keep it to myself and pretend it was nothing, so I told my supervisor. I figured if I was saying things like that in my own head over and over, I probably needed to tell somebody before it got any worse.

But it kept sitting there in my mind, and it got to where I told my supervisor, because I guess I knew I shouldn’t just keep pretending it was nothing. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I usually have little things I fool with, you know? I’ve got tomato plants I’ll mess around with, check on them, pull weeds, see how they’re coming along. I’ve got a bench where I’ll sand wood, just kind of work with my hands, pass the time. My nights have felt off, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it a real sleep problem or just my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing things over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I’ve got things I normally enjoy — little things, but they’re mine, you know? I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, see if they needed water, pull a few weeds. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.\n\nEven food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, it’s gotten in the way, more than I wanted to admit at first.

Work is probably where it showed the clearest. I’m usually the one who keeps things moving, checks behind people, keeps track of what needs doing. But lately I’ve been messing things up, and not just little harmless stuff. My mistakes got serious enough that my supervisor ended up finding me sitting in my truck before dawn, just… sitting there, not going in like I should’ve. And that’s when occupational health got involved. That was embarrassing, honestly. I felt like I’d been exposed somehow, like everybody could see I wasn’t holding it together.

And at home it’s not much better. Bills and normal responsibilities, things I would usually just handle because they need handling, they’ve been piling up. I’ll see something on the counter or know something needs to be paid or dealt with, and I just don’t do it. Then it stacks up, and the more it stacks up, the worse I feel about it, so I avoid it more.

So yeah… it’s not just thoughts floating around. It’s affecting my job, my home, the way I’m showing up for people. I don’t feel like I’m functioning the way I normally do.

Even football, even talking after church, I just don’t have that pull toward it like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I mean, I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that, if that makes sense. Some nights I lay there and it feels like I’m just kind of watching the dark, but I couldn’t tell you exactly how many hours I’m getting or whether it’s every single night.\n\nI know I haven’t felt right lately, but with sleep specifically, I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer. My nights have felt off, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it a real sleep problem or just my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing things over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I’ve got things I normally enjoy — little things, but they’re mine, you know? I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, see if they needed water, pull a few weeds. I’d sit at my bench and sand wood, just work with my hands for a while. I’d watch the Falcons, even if I was fussing at the TV half the game. And after church, I’d usually hang around and talk to folks instead of rushing off.\n\nBut lately it’s like… I look at all that and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I’m supposed to care about, because I always have, but I don’t really get anything from them right now. I’ll stand there by the plants or look at the wood sitting there, and there’s just no pull to it. No pleasure in it like there used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything like that. I just notice it more with eating. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.\n\nEven food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. So I wouldn’t know exactly how many pounds or anything, but my eating’s definitely been off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done than usual over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAt work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.\n\nAnd when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.\n\nGetting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t know if guilty is exactly the word, but maybe it is. I keep going back over my daughter leaving, over and over, like I’m trying to find the one place where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI’ll be sitting there and my mind just starts in — like, maybe I didn’t listen enough, or maybe I was too hard on her, or maybe I missed something plain as day that a better father would’ve seen. And I know people can say grown children make their own choices and all that, and I understand that in my head, but it doesn’t stop me from feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nIt’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. I don’t even know what I think I could fix now, but I keep blaming myself anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about actually harming yourself over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually part of why I’m here.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I’ve been, like, making a plan or anything like that… but I keep having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it. And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.\n\nAt first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. It scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor, because I didn’t really trust myself to just keep pretending it was nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest in activities that you normally find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, no, not really. That’s been one of the stranger parts, because these are things I’ve always had, you know? Little things, but they were mine.\n\nI used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, tie them up right, see if any were coming in. I could spend a good while out there and not even think about it. Lately I’ll look out there and see they need attention, and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I ought to care, but I just… don’t feel much of anything.\n\nSame with my workbench. I used to like sanding wood, getting a piece smooth, just standing there with the dust and the smell of it. That used to settle me some. Now I’ll go out there, pick something up, and just put it back down. It feels like too much, or like there’s no point to it.\n\nEven watching the Falcons — I used to make time for that. I’d holler at the TV like anybody else, get into it. Now it’ll be on and I’m barely watching. I don’t get worked up, don’t really enjoy it.\n\nAnd after church, I used to stay around talking with people, catching up, shaking hands, asking how folks were doing. Lately I just want to leave. Or I stand there and feel like I’m not really part of it. All of that stuff just feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these thoughts of your family being better off without you nearly every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty close to that. I don’t know if I can say I’ve counted every single day, like marked it on a calendar, but it’s been most days, enough that it feels like it’s always sitting right there.\n\nThat thought about them maybe being better off if I didn’t wake up… it keeps coming back. Sometimes it’s first thing in the morning, sometimes at night when everything’s quiet, and sometimes it just hits me in the middle of doing nothing much. And I hate even saying that, because I don’t want to put that on my family, but that’s the thought I’ve been having.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It scared me. I got to where I didn’t want to just keep it to myself and pretend it was nothing, so I told my supervisor. I figured if I was saying things like that in my own head over and over, I probably needed to tell somebody before it got any worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to these thoughts and feelings over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it’s gotten in the way, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is probably where it showed the clearest. I’m usually the one who keeps things moving, checks behind people, keeps track of what needs doing. But lately I’ve been messing things up, and not just little harmless stuff. My mistakes got serious enough that my supervisor ended up finding me sitting in my truck before dawn, just… sitting there, not going in like I should’ve. And that’s when occupational health got involved. That was embarrassing, honestly. I felt like I’d been exposed somehow, like everybody could see I wasn’t holding it together.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. Bills and normal responsibilities, things I would usually just handle because they need handling, they’ve been piling up. I’ll see something on the counter or know something needs to be paid or dealt with, and I just don’t do it. Then it stacks up, and the more it stacks up, the worse I feel about it, so I avoid it more.\n\nSo yeah… it’s not just thoughts floating around. It’s affecting my job, my home, the way I’m showing up for people. I don’t feel like I’m functioning the way I normally do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No… not really. I mean, I might go through the motions a little, but the enjoyment part just isn’t there.

Like I said, those tomato plants used to be something I’d actually look forward to. I’d notice if they needed tying up or if the leaves looked off, and I’d feel some satisfaction taking care of them. Now I look out there and it’s just… nothing. Same with sanding wood at my bench — that used to calm me down, just working with my hands, feeling the wood get smooth. Lately I pick something up and I don’t get any pleasure from it, so I set it right back down.

Even watching the Falcons, which I used to get into, it’s like the game can be on and I’m not really with it. And after church, I used to enjoy hanging around and talking with folks, but now it feels like I’m standing there pretending. It all feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.

I’ll sit and watch the Falcons even when they’re making me mad, and after church I normally stay around and talk to folks.\n\nBut lately… I don’t know. I look at all that stuff and it’s like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I usually care about, but I can’t seem to get myself into them. I’ll stand there looking at the plants or the wood on the bench and just feel nothing much about it. Even football, even talking after church, I just don’t have that pull toward it like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I mean, I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that, if that makes sense. Some nights I lay there and it feels like I’m just kind of watching the dark, but I couldn’t tell you exactly how many hours I’m getting or whether it’s every single night.\n\nI know I haven’t felt right lately, but with sleep specifically, I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer. My nights have felt off, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it a real sleep problem or just my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing things over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I’ve got things I normally enjoy — little things, but they’re mine, you know? I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, see if they needed water, pull a few weeds. I’d sit at my bench and sand wood, just work with my hands for a while. I’d watch the Falcons, even if I was fussing at the TV half the game. And after church, I’d usually hang around and talk to folks instead of rushing off.\n\nBut lately it’s like… I look at all that and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I’m supposed to care about, because I always have, but I don’t really get anything from them right now. I’ll stand there by the plants or look at the wood sitting there, and there’s just no pull to it. No pleasure in it like there used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything like that. I just notice it more with eating. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.\n\nEven food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. So I wouldn’t know exactly how many pounds or anything, but my eating’s definitely been off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done than usual over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAt work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.\n\nAnd when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.\n\nGetting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t know if guilty is exactly the word, but maybe it is. I keep going back over my daughter leaving, over and over, like I’m trying to find the one place where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI’ll be sitting there and my mind just starts in — like, maybe I didn’t listen enough, or maybe I was too hard on her, or maybe I missed something plain as day that a better father would’ve seen. And I know people can say grown children make their own choices and all that, and I understand that in my head, but it doesn’t stop me from feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nIt’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. I don’t even know what I think I could fix now, but I keep blaming myself anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about actually harming yourself over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually part of why I’m here.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I’ve been, like, making a plan or anything like that… but I keep having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it. And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.\n\nAt first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. It scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor, because I didn’t really trust myself to just keep pretending it was nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest in activities that you normally find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, no, not really. That’s been one of the stranger parts, because these are things I’ve always had, you know? Little things, but they were mine.\n\nI used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, tie them up right, see if any were coming in. I could spend a good while out there and not even think about it. Lately I’ll look out there and see they need attention, and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I ought to care, but I just… don’t feel much of anything.\n\nSame with my workbench. I used to like sanding wood, getting a piece smooth, just standing there with the dust and the smell of it. That used to settle me some. Now I’ll go out there, pick something up, and just put it back down. It feels like too much, or like there’s no point to it.\n\nEven watching the Falcons — I used to make time for that. I’d holler at the TV like anybody else, get into it. Now it’ll be on and I’m barely watching. I don’t get worked up, don’t really enjoy it.\n\nAnd after church, I used to stay around talking with people, catching up, shaking hands, asking how folks were doing. Lately I just want to leave. Or I stand there and feel like I’m not really part of it. All of that stuff just feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these thoughts of your family being better off without you nearly every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty close to that. I don’t know if I can say I’ve counted every single day, like marked it on a calendar, but it’s been most days, enough that it feels like it’s always sitting right there.\n\nThat thought about them maybe being better off if I didn’t wake up… it keeps coming back. Sometimes it’s first thing in the morning, sometimes at night when everything’s quiet, and sometimes it just hits me in the middle of doing nothing much. And I hate even saying that, because I don’t want to put that on my family, but that’s the thought I’ve been having.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It scared me. I got to where I didn’t want to just keep it to myself and pretend it was nothing, so I told my supervisor. I figured if I was saying things like that in my own head over and over, I probably needed to tell somebody before it got any worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to these thoughts and feelings over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it’s gotten in the way, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is probably where it showed the clearest. I’m usually the one who keeps things moving, checks behind people, keeps track of what needs doing. But lately I’ve been messing things up, and not just little harmless stuff. My mistakes got serious enough that my supervisor ended up finding me sitting in my truck before dawn, just… sitting there, not going in like I should’ve. And that’s when occupational health got involved. That was embarrassing, honestly. I felt like I’d been exposed somehow, like everybody could see I wasn’t holding it together.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. Bills and normal responsibilities, things I would usually just handle because they need handling, they’ve been piling up. I’ll see something on the counter or know something needs to be paid or dealt with, and I just don’t do it. Then it stacks up, and the more it stacks up, the worse I feel about it, so I avoid it more.\n\nSo yeah… it’s not just thoughts floating around. It’s affecting my job, my home, the way I’m showing up for people. I don’t feel like I’m functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment when doing things that you normally like over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I might go through the motions a little, but the enjoyment part just isn’t there.\n\nLike I said, those tomato plants used to be something I’d actually look forward to. I’d notice if they needed tying up or if the leaves looked off, and I’d feel some satisfaction taking care of them. Now I look out there and it’s just… nothing. Same with sanding wood at my bench — that used to calm me down, just working with my hands, feeling the wood get smooth. Lately I pick something up and I don’t get any pleasure from it, so I set it right back down.\n\nEven watching the Falcons, which I used to get into, it’s like the game can be on and I’m not really with it. And after church, I used to enjoy hanging around and talking with folks, but now it feels like I’m standing there pretending. It all feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I got to where I didn’t want to just keep it to myself and pretend it was nothing, so I told my supervisor. I figured if I was saying things like that in my own head over and over, I probably needed to tell somebody before it got any worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to these thoughts and feelings over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it’s gotten in the way, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is probably where it showed the clearest. I’m usually the one who keeps things moving, checks behind people, keeps track of what needs doing. But lately I’ve been messing things up, and not just little harmless stuff. My mistakes got serious enough that my supervisor ended up finding me sitting in my truck before dawn, just… sitting there, not going in like I should’ve. And that’s when occupational health got involved. That was embarrassing, honestly. I felt like I’d been exposed somehow, like everybody could see I wasn’t holding it together.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. Bills and normal responsibilities, things I would usually just handle because they need handling, they’ve been piling up. I’ll see something on the counter or know something needs to be paid or dealt with, and I just don’t do it. Then it stacks up, and the more it stacks up, the worse I feel about it, so I avoid it more.\n\nSo yeah… it’s not just thoughts floating around. It’s affecting my job, my home, the way I’m showing up for people. I don’t feel like I’m functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment when doing things that you normally like over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I might go through the motions a little, but the enjoyment part just isn’t there.\n\nLike I said, those tomato plants used to be something I’d actually look forward to. I’d notice if they needed tying up or if the leaves looked off, and I’d feel some satisfaction taking care of them. Now I look out there and it’s just… nothing. Same with sanding wood at my bench — that used to calm me down, just working with my hands, feeling the wood get smooth. Lately I pick something up and I don’t get any pleasure from it, so I set it right back down.\n\nEven watching the Falcons, which I used to get into, it’s like the game can be on and I’m not really with it. And after church, I used to enjoy hanging around and talking with folks, but now it feels like I’m standing there pretending. It all feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say these thoughts of your family being better off without you are present nearly every day, or are they intermittent?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

It’s… it feels pretty close to nearly every day. I don’t want to say every single day like I’ve been keeping a perfect record, but it’s not just once in a while either.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… um, I’m here because I scared myself, honestly. I’ve been having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Not even saying it out loud feels right, but that’s what’s been going through my head.\n\nI tried to keep it to myself at first, because I don’t like making a big thing out of things, and I’m not real used to talking about this kind of stuff. But it kept sitting there in my mind, and it got to where I told my supervisor, because I guess I knew I shouldn’t just keep pretending it was nothing. That’s what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: It’s been about two weeks now, I’d say. It kind of started after my daughter left Georgia for the military. That was when I noticed this whole change in me, like something shifted, and those thoughts started coming in after that. It hasn’t been years or anything like that, but these last couple weeks have felt pretty heavy.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things you normally like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me, because I usually have little things I fool with, you know? I’ve got tomato plants I’ll mess around with, check on them, pull weeds, see how they’re coming along. I’ve got a bench where I’ll sand wood, just kind of work with my hands, pass the time. I’ll sit and watch the Falcons even when they’re making me mad, and after church I normally stay around and talk to folks.\n\nBut lately… I don’t know. I look at all that stuff and it’s like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I usually care about, but I can’t seem to get myself into them. I’ll stand there looking at the plants or the wood on the bench and just feel nothing much about it. Even football, even talking after church, I just don’t have that pull toward it like I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any problems with your sleep over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Sleep… I mean, I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track of it like that, if that makes sense. Some nights I lay there and it feels like I’m just kind of watching the dark, but I couldn’t tell you exactly how many hours I’m getting or whether it’s every single night.\n\nI know I haven’t felt right lately, but with sleep specifically, I’m not sure I can give you a clean answer. My nights have felt off, I guess, but I don’t know if I’d call it a real sleep problem or just my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing things over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I mean, I’ve got things I normally enjoy — little things, but they’re mine, you know? I used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, see if they needed water, pull a few weeds. I’d sit at my bench and sand wood, just work with my hands for a while. I’d watch the Falcons, even if I was fussing at the TV half the game. And after church, I’d usually hang around and talk to folks instead of rushing off.\n\nBut lately it’s like… I look at all that and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. Like I know those are things I’m supposed to care about, because I always have, but I don’t really get anything from them right now. I’ll stand there by the plants or look at the wood sitting there, and there’s just no pull to it. No pleasure in it like there used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything like that. I just notice it more with eating. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites, like I’ll stand there and know I ought to eat something, but nothing sounds good.\n\nEven food I normally like — like if somebody brings something into the warehouse, or I’ve got leftovers at home that I’d usually be glad to have — it just sits there. I’ll look at it, maybe take a little bit, and then it ends up staying in the fridge until I throw it out. So I wouldn’t know exactly how many pounds or anything, but my eating’s definitely been off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done than usual over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I have noticed that. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAt work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.\n\nAnd when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.\n\nGetting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t know if guilty is exactly the word, but maybe it is. I keep going back over my daughter leaving, over and over, like I’m trying to find the one place where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI’ll be sitting there and my mind just starts in — like, maybe I didn’t listen enough, or maybe I was too hard on her, or maybe I missed something plain as day that a better father would’ve seen. And I know people can say grown children make their own choices and all that, and I understand that in my head, but it doesn’t stop me from feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nIt’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. I don’t even know what I think I could fix now, but I keep blaming myself anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about actually harming yourself over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually part of why I’m here.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I’ve been, like, making a plan or anything like that… but I keep having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it. And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.\n\nAt first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. It scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor, because I didn’t really trust myself to just keep pretending it was nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest in activities that you normally find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, no, not really. That’s been one of the stranger parts, because these are things I’ve always had, you know? Little things, but they were mine.\n\nI used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, tie them up right, see if any were coming in. I could spend a good while out there and not even think about it. Lately I’ll look out there and see they need attention, and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I ought to care, but I just… don’t feel much of anything.\n\nSame with my workbench. I used to like sanding wood, getting a piece smooth, just standing there with the dust and the smell of it. That used to settle me some. Now I’ll go out there, pick something up, and just put it back down. It feels like too much, or like there’s no point to it.\n\nEven watching the Falcons — I used to make time for that. I’d holler at the TV like anybody else, get into it. Now it’ll be on and I’m barely watching. I don’t get worked up, don’t really enjoy it.\n\nAnd after church, I used to stay around talking with people, catching up, shaking hands, asking how folks were doing. Lately I just want to leave. Or I stand there and feel like I’m not really part of it. All of that stuff just feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these thoughts of your family being better off without you nearly every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty close to that. I don’t know if I can say I’ve counted every single day, like marked it on a calendar, but it’s been most days, enough that it feels like it’s always sitting right there.\n\nThat thought about them maybe being better off if I didn’t wake up… it keeps coming back. Sometimes it’s first thing in the morning, sometimes at night when everything’s quiet, and sometimes it just hits me in the middle of doing nothing much. And I hate even saying that, because I don’t want to put that on my family, but that’s the thought I’ve been having.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It scared me. I got to where I didn’t want to just keep it to myself and pretend it was nothing, so I told my supervisor. I figured if I was saying things like that in my own head over and over, I probably needed to tell somebody before it got any worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to these thoughts and feelings over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it’s gotten in the way, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is probably where it showed the clearest. Or I guess other people have noticed it too, maybe even before I wanted to admit it.\n\nAt work, folks keep asking me if I’m sick or if I’m feeling okay, because I’m just… slow. Like I’ll be walking across the floor, and it feels like everybody else is moving normal and I’m dragging myself along. My legs feel heavy, like I’m trying to walk through mud or something. Not pain exactly, just like everything takes more effort than it ought to.\n\nAnd when somebody asks me something simple — something I should be able to answer quick, because I’ve done that job long enough — I’ll just kind of stand there. I hear the question, I know I’m supposed to answer, but it takes me forever to get the words out or even sort out what they’re asking. Then I can see them looking at me like, “What’s wrong with him?” That bothers me.\n\nGetting things done has been harder too. Even regular tasks feel like they take twice as long. It’s not that I don’t know how to do them. It’s more like my body and my head are both moving too slow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t know if guilty is exactly the word, but maybe it is. I keep going back over my daughter leaving, over and over, like I’m trying to find the one place where I should’ve done something different.\n\nI’ll be sitting there and my mind just starts in — like, maybe I didn’t listen enough, or maybe I was too hard on her, or maybe I missed something plain as day that a better father would’ve seen. And I know people can say grown children make their own choices and all that, and I understand that in my head, but it doesn’t stop me from feeling like I failed her somehow.\n\nIt’s like there’s this voice saying, “If you’d been better, if you’d handled it right, she wouldn’t have left like that.” I keep replaying things I said, things I didn’t say, times I could’ve called or asked more questions. I don’t even know what I think I could fix now, but I keep blaming myself anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about actually harming yourself over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s actually part of why I’m here.\n\nI don’t know that I’d say I’ve been, like, making a plan or anything like that… but I keep having these thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Like if I went to sleep and that was it. And I know that sounds awful to say out loud, especially about your own family, but it keeps coming into my head.\n\nAt first I tried to brush it off, like maybe I was just tired or having a bad day, but it kept coming back. I’d be sitting there thinking they’d have less to worry about, less disappointment, less of me being like this. And then I got scared because that’s not how I usually think. It scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor, because I didn’t really trust myself to just keep pretending it was nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any interest in activities that you normally find enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, no, not really. That’s been one of the stranger parts, because these are things I’ve always had, you know? Little things, but they were mine.\n\nI used to go out and mess with my tomato plants, check the leaves, tie them up right, see if any were coming in. I could spend a good while out there and not even think about it. Lately I’ll look out there and see they need attention, and it’s like I’m looking at somebody else’s yard. I know I ought to care, but I just… don’t feel much of anything.\n\nSame with my workbench. I used to like sanding wood, getting a piece smooth, just standing there with the dust and the smell of it. That used to settle me some. Now I’ll go out there, pick something up, and just put it back down. It feels like too much, or like there’s no point to it.\n\nEven watching the Falcons — I used to make time for that. I’d holler at the TV like anybody else, get into it. Now it’ll be on and I’m barely watching. I don’t get worked up, don’t really enjoy it.\n\nAnd after church, I used to stay around talking with people, catching up, shaking hands, asking how folks were doing. Lately I just want to leave. Or I stand there and feel like I’m not really part of it. All of that stuff just feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these thoughts of your family being better off without you nearly every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty close to that. I don’t know if I can say I’ve counted every single day, like marked it on a calendar, but it’s been most days, enough that it feels like it’s always sitting right there.\n\nThat thought about them maybe being better off if I didn’t wake up… it keeps coming back. Sometimes it’s first thing in the morning, sometimes at night when everything’s quiet, and sometimes it just hits me in the middle of doing nothing much. And I hate even saying that, because I don’t want to put that on my family, but that’s the thought I’ve been having.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. It scared me. I got to where I didn’t want to just keep it to myself and pretend it was nothing, so I told my supervisor. I figured if I was saying things like that in my own head over and over, I probably needed to tell somebody before it got any worse.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning due to these thoughts and feelings over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it’s gotten in the way, more than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork is probably where it showed the clearest. I’m usually the one who keeps things moving, checks behind people, keeps track of what needs doing. But lately I’ve been messing things up, and not just little harmless stuff. My mistakes got serious enough that my supervisor ended up finding me sitting in my truck before dawn, just… sitting there, not going in like I should’ve. And that’s when occupational health got involved. That was embarrassing, honestly. I felt like I’d been exposed somehow, like everybody could see I wasn’t holding it together.\n\nAnd at home it’s not much better. Bills and normal responsibilities, things I would usually just handle because they need handling, they’ve been piling up. I’ll see something on the counter or know something needs to be paid or dealt with, and I just don’t do it. Then it stacks up, and the more it stacks up, the worse I feel about it, so I avoid it more.\n\nSo yeah… it’s not just thoughts floating around. It’s affecting my job, my home, the way I’m showing up for people. I don’t feel like I’m functioning the way I normally do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt any pleasure or enjoyment when doing things that you normally like over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, I might go through the motions a little, but the enjoyment part just isn’t there.\n\nLike I said, those tomato plants used to be something I’d actually look forward to. I’d notice if they needed tying up or if the leaves looked off, and I’d feel some satisfaction taking care of them. Now I look out there and it’s just… nothing. Same with sanding wood at my bench — that used to calm me down, just working with my hands, feeling the wood get smooth. Lately I pick something up and I don’t get any pleasure from it, so I set it right back down.\n\nEven watching the Falcons, which I used to get into, it’s like the game can be on and I’m not really with it. And after church, I used to enjoy hanging around and talking with folks, but now it feels like I’m standing there pretending. It all feels like it belongs to somebody else right now.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these thoughts of your family being better off without you are present nearly every day, or are they intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it feels pretty close to nearly every day. I don’t want to say every single day like I’ve been keeping a perfect record, but it’s not just once in a while either.\n\nThat thought keeps coming back — that my family might be better off if I didn’t wake up. And I know how awful that sounds. I don’t like saying it out loud. But it’s been there enough, and strong enough, that it scared me. That’s really what pushed me to come in. I ended up telling my supervisor because I didn’t feel right keeping it to myself anymore. I’m not sure I can pin it down cleanly like that. It’s not like I’ve been watching the clock and saying, “Okay, this started at 8 and lifted at 3.” It kind of runs together on me.

I can say there are moments where I’m distracted enough that it’s not right in my face — like if someone is talking directly to me or I’m forced to handle something right then. But I don’t know that I’d call that feeling better, exactly. More like I’m just not thinking about it for a minute.

Most days it feels like it’s hanging over me in the background, even when I’m doing something else. Sometimes it gets heavier, and sometimes it eases up a little, but I haven’t really noticed many times lately where I feel like myself and think, “Oh, I’m okay now.” It’s more like I’m carrying it around and just trying to get through the day.
